\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
% Started as https://cs.stackexchange.com/questions/69197/difference-between-turing-machine-and-universal-turing-machine
Your friend asks,
``What can a Universal Turing machine do that a regular Turing machine 
cannot?''
Help them out.
\begin{answer}
A Universal Turing machine is a regular machine, in that it is just another
machine in the list $\TM_0$, $TM_1$, \ldots\@

Perhaps your friend's question was meant to be more like, ``What does a 
Universal Turing machine
do that a non-Universal machine does not?''
Every machine does a job.
Some machines expect a single input and then double it, 
some expect two inputs and then add them.
A Universal Turing machine expects two inputs, $e$ and~$x$,
and then the output of this machine is the same as the output
of the machine $\TM_e$ on input~$x$
(including failing to halt if that machine fails to halt).
It is `Universal' in that any input/output behavior that can be mechanically 
produced can be produced by this one device.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Has a Universal Turing machine, or a machine equivalent to one, 
ever been built?
\begin{answer}
Every general purpose computer, such as a standard laptop or a cell phone,
is equivalent in computable power to a Universal Turing machine.

A person can worry that a standard laptop for instance
does not come installed with infinitely much memory.
That's true.
But such a machine can store things in the Cloud and so is not restricted to
the installed memory, and that memory in this way is unbounded.

A person can go on to worry that there are only so many bits available in the
physical universe, which might be so, and thus memory is not in the end 
unbounded.
Perhaps that is true\Dash although we could worry back that we have enough time
to exhaust what bits there are before the Big Bang becomes a Big Crunch\Dash 
but we will take
general purpose devices to be at the least so very close to 
Universal Turing machines that it is quite hard to tell them apart.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Can a Universal Turing machine similate another Universal Turing machine,
or itself?
\begin{answer}
Yes.
If $\TM_{e_0}$ and $\TM_{e_1}$ are Universal then giving the first one
the inputs $e_1$ and~$x$ will result in the input/output behavior
exactly like the second.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} % From https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/41854/67754
Your class has a jerk who keeps throwing out pronouncements
that the prof has to patiently correct.
This time its, 
``Universal Turing machines make no sense.
How can a machine simulate another machine if it has a different alphabet?
Obviously it can't.
And how could a machine simulate another machine that has more states?
It makes no sense at all.''
Clue this chucklehead in.
\begin{answer}
The answer is that it does those things in software.
Your laptop uses the binary alphabet, but simulates working with
any language, with any alphabet.
For instace, your laptop may simulate \str{a} with \str{111101}.
Similarly, a Universal Turing machine with alphabet 
$\Sigma=\set{\str{B},\str{1}}$ can simulate a Turing machine with
alphabet  $\hat{\Sigma}=\set{\str{B},\str{a},\str{b}}$ by having
codes for each symbol.
Likewise, the universal machine does not use its states to simulate the states
of the simulated machine.
Rather, it has codes for those states that it manipulates on the tape.

Chucklehead.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there more than one Universal Turing machine?
\begin{answer}
Yes, every computable function has infinitely many 
indices, by~\cref{lem:EveryCompFcnHasInfManyIndices}.
So if $\TM_e$ is universal, then the associated function~$\TMfcn_e$
has infinitely many indices:~ there are infinitely many
unequal indices so that
$\TMfcn_e=\TMfcn_{e_0}=\TMfcn_{e_1}=\cdots\,$.
The associated machines 
$\TM_{e_0}$, $\TM_{e_1}$, \ldots{} are all universal.
\end{answer}

\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Consider the function $f(x_0,x_1)=3x_0+x_0\cdot x_1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Freeze $x_0$ to have the value~$4$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item Freeze $x_0$ at~$5$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item Freeeze $x_1$ to be~$0$.
  What is the resulting function?  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The one-variable function is $f_{0,4}(x_1)=12+4x_1$. 
\item The one-variable function is $f_{0,5}(x_1)=15+5x_1$. 
\item The one-variable function is $f_{1,0}(x_0)=3x_0$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider
$f(x_0,x_1,x_2)=x_0+2x_1+3x_2$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Freeze $x_0$ to have the value~$1$.
  What is the resulting two-variable function?
\item What two-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be $2$?
\item Let $a$ be a natural number.
  What two-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be $a$?
\item Freeze $x_0$ at~$5$ and $x_1$ at~$3$.
  What is the resulting one-variable function?
\item What one-variable function results from fixing $x_0$ to be~$a$
  and $x_1$ to be~$b$, for $a,b\in\N$?
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Consider the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1000.pdf}
\end{center}
Imagine that it has index~$e$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Draw the flowchart for the Turing machine $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$. 
  Also describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(2)$?
\item Draw the flowchart for $\TM_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}$. 
  Also describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,0}e,0}(2)$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The two subscripts on the function $s_{1,1}$ indicate that
  the flowchart freezes the first one input, and leaves the second one as
  a variable.
  The arguments $e$ and~$1$ indicate that it works on Turing machine~$e$,
  the one whose flow chart is given, and that the input is frozen to be
  $x_0=1$.
  The result is this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1001.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This is the function, the input/output behavior resulting from 
  that Turing machine: $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(x_1)=1+x_1$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(0)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(1)=2$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(2)=3$.
\item The notation $s_{1,1}(e,0)$ indicates that
  the flowchart freezes the first one input, leaves the second one as
  a variable, 
  that it works on Turing machine~$e$, and that the input is frozen to be
  $x_0=0$.
  The result is this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1002.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This is the function, the input/output behavior resulting from 
  that Turing machine: $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(x_1)=0+x_1=x_1$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(0)=0$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(1)=1$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(2)=2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1003.pdf}
\end{center}
Imagine that it has index~$e$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Draw the flowchart for the Turing machine $\TM_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}$. 
  Also describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(0,1)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(1,0)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(2,3)$?
\item Draw the flowchart for $\TM_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}$. 
  Also describe the function $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}$.
\item What are the values of $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(0)$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(1)$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(2)$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The notation $s_{1,2}(e,1)$ indicates that
  the flowchart freezes the first one input, leaves the second 
  and third ones as variables,
  that it works on Turing machine~$e$, and that the input is frozen to be
  $x_0=1$.
  The result is this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1004.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This is the function, the input/output behavior resulting from 
  that Turing machine: $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(x_1,x_2)=1+x_1\cdot x_2$.
\item We have $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(0,1)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(1,0)=1$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{1,2}(e,1)}(2,3)=7$.
\item The $s_{2,1}(e,1,2)$ indicates that we should
  freeze the first and second inputs, leave the third input as
  a variable,
  that it works on Turing machine~$e$, and that the inputs are frozen to be
  $x_0=1$ and~$x_1=2$.
  The result is this flowchart.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1005.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This is the function, the input/output behavior resulting from 
  that Turing machine: $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(x_2)=1+2\cdot x_2$.
\item The values are $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(0)=1$, 
  $\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(1)=3$, and~$\TMfcn_{s_{2,1}(e,1,2)}(2)=5$?
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1006.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}$ by giving the flowchart.
\item What is the value of $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(5)$?
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}$.
\item What is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(5)$?
\item Describe $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}$.
\item What is $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(5)$?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(x_1)=x_1$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,0)}(5)=5$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(x_1)=x_1$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,1)}(5)=5$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(x_1)\diverges$
\item $\TMfcn_{s_{1,1}(e,2)}(5)\diverges$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Suppose that the Turing machine sketched by this flowchart has index~$e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1012.pdf}
\end{center}
We will describe the family of functions parametrized by the arguments~$x_0$
and~$x_1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Use \cref{th:smn}, the Parameter Theorem, to fix $x_0=0$ and~$x_1=3$.
  Give a flowchart for $\TM_{s(e,0,3)}$ and describe $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}$.
  What is  $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}(5)$?
\item Use the Parameter Theorem to fix $x_0=1$.
  Give a flowchart for $\TM_{s(e,1,3)}$ and describe $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}$.
  What is  $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}(5)$?
\item Give a flowchart for $\TM_{s(e,a,b)}$ and describe $\TMfcn_{s(e,a,b)}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This is the flowchart sketching the machine $\TM_{s(e,0,3)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1013.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This is the function that it computes is $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}(y)=3y$.
  Consequently,  $\TMfcn_{s(e,0,3)}(5)=15$.
\item This is the flowchart for $\TM_{s(e,1,3)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1014.pdf}
  \end{center}
  This machine computes the function $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}(y)=3+y$
  and consequently  $\TMfcn_{s(e,1,3)}(5)=8$.
\item This is the flowchart for $\TM_{s(e,a,b)}$.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts1015.pdf}
  \end{center}
  The distinction between this and the flowchart in the question statement
  is that here the machine does not read in~$x_0$ or~$x_1$.  
  Rather, they are hard-coded, as the values $a$ and~$b$, 
  into the program body.
  This is a family of functions.
  It is parametrized by~$a$ and~$b$, and the indices of these functions are
  uniformily computable from $e$, $a$, and~$b$, with the function~$s$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

%================================================
\section{Unsolvability}
We've showed that
there are functions that are not mechanically computable.
We gave a counting argument, that there are countably many Turing machines but 
uncountably many functions and so there are functions with no
associated machine.
While knowing what's true is great,
our taste is to prefer to construct a specific 
unsolvable function rather than just to
know that there is one.
We will now do that.



\subsection{The \HP}
The natural approach is to go through 
Cantor's Theorem and effectivize it,
to see how much of the proof we can express as a construction.

Consider an illustrative table adapted from the discussion of
Cantor's Theorem on page~\pageref{ex:DiagonalizationTable}.
This table's rows are the computable functions and 
its columns are the inputs.
For instance, this table imagines that $\phi_2(3)=5$.
\begin{center} \small \label{HPDiagonalizationTable}
  %<*table:CantorThmEffectivization>
  \setlength{\arraycolsep}{0.75\arraycolsep}
  \begin{tabular}{r|ccccccc@{\hspace*{1em}$\ldots$}}
    \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ } 
    &\multicolumn{7}{c}{\text{\tabulartext{Input~$x$}}}  \\
     % \multicolumn{1}{c}{\text{\tabulartext{Function}}}
     \multicolumn{1}{c}{\ }
    &$0$ &$1$ &$2$ &$3$ &$4$ &$5$ &$6$ \\ 
   \cline{2-8}
   \rule{0em}{2.15ex}% bit more vertical room 
   $\phi_0$  &$3$  &$1$  &$2$  &$7$  &$7$  &$0$  &$4$  \\  
   $\phi_1$  &$0$  &$5$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  &$0$  \\  
   $\phi_2$  &$1$  &$4$  &$1$  &$5$  &$9$  &$2$  &$6$  \\  
   $\phi_3$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  &$1$  &$9$  \\  
   $\phi_4$  &$1$  &$0$  &$1$  &$0$  &$0$  &$1$  &$0$  \\
   $\phi_5$  &$6$  &$2$  &$5$  &$5$  &$4$  &$1$  &$8$  \\
   \multicolumn{1}{r}{$\vdotswithin{0}$} 
    &\multicolumn{6}{c}{$\vdotswithin{0}$}  
  \end{tabular}
  %</table:CantorThmEffectivization>
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.75fil}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp02.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*discussion:HP>
Diagonalize.
That is, consider the program on the right.
Its output seems not to be any of the table's rows,
which appears to contradict the supposition that
the rows are all of the computable functions.

In the Cantor's Theorem argument, the contradiction showed that there are more
than countably many rows.
But here we know that there are countably many Turing machines 
so we can imagine the rows that are shown.
So what's the resolution of the apparent contradicion?
The program's first, second, fourth and fifth boxes are trivial so the
problem must lie with getting through its third box.

The issue is halting\Dash among the computable functions 
there are ones does not converge on some inputs.  
In particular the appraent contradiction shows that 
this must happen on the diagonal; there must be
an $e\in\N$ so that $\phi_e(e)\diverges$.
Recognizing this makes the difficulty dissolve because for those input~$e$'s
in the flowchart, the program will never leave 
the middle box and so will never get to the contradictory output box.
In short,
in the above table some of the entries must be up arrows, $\uparrow$.
%</discussion:HP>

%<*def:K>
\begin{definition}
  $\definend{K}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$, that is, $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}}$
\end{definition}
%</def:K>

%<*prob:HP>
\begin{problem}[Halting Problem]  \label{prob:HaltingProblem}
Given~$e\in\N$, determine whether Turing machine~$\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$,
that is, whether $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:HP>

Certainly for any $e\in\N$, either $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$ or
$\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$.
Thus there is a function giving~$1$ in the first case and~$0$ in the second.
The \HP{} is to decide if that function is computable, 
to find a Turing machine with that behavior.
% That is, the \HP{} is to produce a program 
% $\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$ that acts as the characteristic
% function of $K$, returning $1$ if $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$ and
% returning~$0$ otherwise.

%<*th:HPIsUnsolvable>
\begin{theorem}[Unsolvability of the \HP]
The \HP{} is mechanically unsolvable.
\end{theorem}
%</th:HPIsUnsolvable>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:HPIsUnsolvablei>
Assume otherwise, that there is a Turing machine whose behavior is this.
\begin{equation*}
  K(e)=
  \computablefunction{halt\_checker}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
  1  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}  \\
  0  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Then the function below is also mechanically computable.
The flowchart illustrates how $f$ is constructed;
it uses $\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$ in its decision box.
%</pf:HPIsUnsolvablei>
\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.3\textwidth}
  \begin{equation*}
  f(e)=
    \begin{cases}
    0    &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\diverges$}  \\
    \uparrow  &\case{if $\TMfcn_e(e)\converges$}
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
\end{minipage}
\hspace{2.5em}
\vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/flowcharts/flowcharts02.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:HPIsUnsolvableii>
(In $f$'s first case the output value doesn't matter,
all that matters is that~$f$ converges.)
Since this function is mechanically computable it has an index.
Let that index be~$\hat{e}$, so that
$f(x)=\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(x)$ for all inputs~$x$.

Now consider $f(\hat{e})=\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}(\hat{e})$,
that is, feed the machine on the right the input~$\hat{e}$.
If it diverges then by the first clause in the definition of~$f$
we have $f(\hat{e})\converges$,
which contradicts divergence.
If it converges then by $f$'s second clause 
we have $f(\hat{e})\diverges$, also impossible.
Since assuming that $\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$ is 
mechanically computable leads to a contradiction,
$\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$
is not mechanically computable.
%</pf:HPIsUnsolvableii>
\end{proof}

With Church's Thesis in mind will say that a task is \definend{unsolvable} 
if it is mechanically unsolvable, if no Turing machine 
computes that task.
If the task is to compute the answer to `yes' or `no' questions,
such as the task of determining membership in a set,
then we will also use as a synonym that the problem is \definend{undecidable}.




\subsection{Discussion}
The fact that the Halting Problem is unsolvable does not mean that 
we cannot tell if any program halts. 
This program obviously halts for every input.
\begin{lstlisting}
#;1> (define (prompt/read prompt)
--->     (display prompt)
--->     (read-line))
#;2> 
#;2> (+ 1
--->    (string->number (prompt/read "Enter n")))
Enter n---> 4
5  
\end{lstlisting}
Nor does the unsolvability of \HP{} mean we cannot tell if a program 
does not halt.
Consider this one.
\begin{lstlisting}
#;1> (define (f x)
--->   (+ 1 (f x)))
\end{lstlisting}
Once we start it, it just keeps going.
\begin{lstlisting}
#;2> (f 0)
^C
	Call history:

	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	<eval>	  [f] (f x)
	<eval>	  [f] (+ 1 (f x))
	readline.scm:392: print-call-chain	  	<--

*** user interrupt ***
\end{lstlisting}
\noindent Instead, the unsolvability of the Halting Problem 
says that there is no single program that, for all~$e$, correctly computes 
in a finite time whether $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$.

That has the qualifier `finite time' 
because we could perfectly well write this program
\begin{lstlisting}
read e
simulate machine e on input e
print 1
\end{lstlisting}
but if $\TM_e$ on input~$e$ fails to halt then we would not find out 
in a finite time.
The `single program' qualifier is there because for any index~$e$,
either $\TM_e$ halts on~$e$ or else it does not.
That is, for any~$e$ one of these two programs
gives the right answer.
\begin{center}
% \vspace*{-4ex}
\vspace*{-\ht\strutbox}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
read e
print 0
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
\hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
\begin{minipage}[t]{0.4\linewidth}%
\begin{lstlisting}
read e
print 1
\end{lstlisting}%
\end{minipage}%
% \vspace*{-2ex}
\end{center}
Of course, solving the \HP{} by guessing is not what we had in mind.
We had in mind a program, an effective procedure, that inputs~$e$
and outputs an answer in a finite time.

Thus, the unsolvability of the Halting Problem is about 
the non-existence of a single program that works across all
indices.
It speaks to uniformity,
or rather, the impossibility of uniformity.




\subsection{Significance}  \label{subsec:Significance}
A beginning programming class could leave the 
impression that if a program doesn't halt then
it just has a bug, something fixable.
So the \HP{} could seem to be not very interesting. 
That impression is wrong.

Imagine 
a utility for programmers, $\computablefunction{always\_halt}$,
to patch non-halting programs.
After writing a program~$\TM$ we run it through the utility, which
modifies the source so that for any input where $\TM$ fails to halt,
the modified program will halt (and output $0$) but the utility
does not change any outputs where $\TM$ does halt.
That would cause the list of all programs to give rise to a 
list of total functions like the one on \pageref{HPDiagonalizationTable},
and diagonalization gives a contradiction.

Thus, halting, or rather failure to halt, is central
to the nature of computation.
In any general computational scheme
there must be some computations that halt on all inputs, 
some that halt on no inputs, 
and some that \citetext{halt on some inputs but not on others}{A 
  Turing machine could fail to halt because it has an infinite loop.
  The Turing machine
  $\TM_0=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{B}{0},
    \tminstruction{0}{1}{1}{0}}$
  never halts, cycling forever in state $q_0$.
  We could patch this problem; we could write a program
  $\computablefunction{inf\_loop\_checker}$ that at each
  step checks whether a machine has ever before in this computation
  had the same configuration as it has now.
  This program will detect infinite loops like the prior one.
  
  However, note that there are machines that fail to halt but
  do not have loops, in the sense that they never repeat a configuration.
  One is $\TM_1=\set{\tminstruction{0}{B}{1}{1},
        \tminstruction{1}{1}{R}{0}}$
  which when started on a blank tape will endlessly move to the right,
  writing \str{1}'s.}.

That alone is enough to justify study of the \HP{} but we will give a 
second reason.
If $\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$ were a computable function
then we could solve many problems that we currently don't know how to solve. 

\margingraphicnomag*[l]{.425\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp00.pdf}
For instance, a natural number is \textit{perfect} if it is the 
sum of its proper positive divisors.
Thus $6$ is perfect because $6=1+2+3$.
Similarly, $28 = 1 + 2 + 4 + 7 + 14$ is perfect.
% and after that comes $496$ and~$8128$.
These were studied as far back as Euclid and
today we 
\citetext{understand the form of all even perfect numbers}{A 
number is an even perfect number if and only if it has the form 
$(2^p-1)\cdot 2^{p-1}$ where $2^p-1$ is prime.}.
But we do not know if there are any odd perfect numbers.

With a solution to the Halting Problem we could settle that question.
The
program shown here searches for an odd perfect number.\footnote{%
  It takes an
  input $x$ but ignores it; in this book we have a convention that 
  we like Turing machines to all take an input and all give an output.}
If it finds one then it halts.
If not then it does not halt.
So giving $\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$ the index of this program
would settle whether there exists an odd perfect number.
There are many open questions 
\citetext{involving an unbounded search}{%
  A computer program that solved 
  the Halting Problem, if one existed, could be very slow. 
  So this might not be a feasible way to settle this question.  
  But at the moment we are studying what can be done in principle.} 
that would fall to this approach.
(Just to name one more:~no one knows if there is any $n>4$ such that
$2^{(2^n)}+1$ is prime.
We could answer the question by writing $\TM$ to search for such an~$n$,
 and give the index of $\TM$ to 
$\computablefunction{halt\_checker}$.)
 
Before moving on, we note the appearance here of the theme of unbounded search.
It does not just appear in looking for perfect numbers;
we have seen it before, in defining $\mu$-recursion.
And it is at the heart of the \HP{} since
the obvious way to test whether $\phi_e(e)\converges$
is to run a brute force computation, an unbounded
search for a stage at which the computation halts.    







% \begin{exercises}
% \begin{exercise}
% True of false: there is no function that solves the Halting Problem:
% there is no $f$ such that $f(e)=1$ if $\phi_e(e)\converges$ and $f(e)=0$ if
% $\phi_e(e)\diverges$.
% \begin{answer}
% False.  
% There is such a function.  
% But it is not computable; there is no Turing machine whose behavior is
% that function.
% \end{answer}

% \end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}  \label{ex:HPByGetTotFcns}
% Make precise the table argument in \cref{subsec:Significance} 
% by showing that if $\computablefunction{halt\_check}$ were computable 
% then we could write a program that behaves 
% like $\phi_e$ except that for any inputs~$x$ where $\phi_e(x)\diverges$,
% this program outputs the nominal value~$0$.
% Diagonalize to get a contradiction.
% \begin{answer}
% If 
% $\computablefunction{halt\_check}$ were computable then we could  
% write the program shown below.
% \begin{center}
%   \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp01.pdf}
% \end{center}
% It behaves 
% like $\phi_e$ except that for any inputs~$x$ where $\phi_e(x)\diverges$
% this program outputs~$0$.
% With this program we get an input-output relationship like that imagined
% in the \cref{subsec:Significance}'s table, 
% containing only computable functions that are total.
% If $\computablefunction{halt\_check}$ is computable then
% this list is also computable and every total function is one of its rows,
% since if a function $\phi_e$ is total then the flowchart just never 
% takes the `\texttt{No}' branch.
% Now we can diagonalize, giving a contradiction.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}
% \end{exercises}







%================================================
\subsection{General unsolvabilty}
The \HP{} is part of a larger unsolvability phenomenon.
% We will consider a number of problems and for each
% show that if we could solve it then we
% could solve the \HP, and consequently 
% the given problem is also unsolvable.

\begin{example} \label{ex:HaltOnThree}
Consider the problem of determining whether a given Turing machine halts 
on the input~$3$.
That is, given~$x$, does $\TMfcn_x(3)\converges$?
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TM_x$ halts on input~$3$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
We will show 
that if $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$ 
were a computable function then we could solve the \HP.
% \margingraphicnomag*[r]{.3\textwidth}{background/asy/hp/hp06.pdf}

The plan is to create a system where being able to determine
whether a program halts on~$3$ allows us to settle \HP{} questions.
Consider the program below on the right.
It reads the input~$y$ and ignores it, and has a nominal output statement.
The action is in the middle box, where it 
simulates running $\TM_x$ on input~$x$.
If that simulation halts then the program as a whole halts.
If that simulation does not halt then this program as a whole
does not halt.
Thus, the program on the right halts on input~$y=3$ if and only if 
$\TM_x$ halts on~$x$.
So with this flowchart we could leverage being able
to answer questions about
halting on~$3$ to answer
questions about whether $\TM_x$ halts on~$x$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp06.pdf}
  \hspace*{4em}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp07.pdf}
\end{center}
% But we don't want to only determine whether $\TM_x$ 
% halts on~$x$ for a single~$x$.
% For that we will bring in uniformity, using the program on the left.

With that motivation we are ready for the argument.
For the proof that
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$ is not mechanically
computable, assume otherwise.
Consider this function.
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    42 &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Observe that~$\psi$ is mechanically computable, because it
is computed by the flowchart above on the left.
So by Church's Thesis
there is a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
That Turing machine has some index~$e$, meaning that $\psi=\phi_e$.

Use the \smn{} theorem to parametrize~$x$, giving $\phi_{s(e,x)}$.
This is a family of functions, one for~$x=0$, one for~$x=1$, etc.\@
Below is the family of associated machines.
Each has a `Read~$y$' but no `Read~$x$'; rather, 
the value used in the middle box is hard coded into each source.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp15.pdf}}
  \hspace*{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp16.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2em}\ldots{}\hspace*{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp07.pdf}}
  \hspace*{2em}\ldots{}
\end{center}

As in the plan above, for all $x\in\N$ we have that
$\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}(s(e,x))=1$.
We know that the \smn{} function~$s$ is computable so 
the supposition that $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$ is 
also computable gives that the composition
$\composed{\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}}{s}$
is computable, which 
in turn gives that the \HP{} is 
computable, 
which is false.
We conclude that $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$
is not mechanically computable.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:OutputsSeven}
Show that this function is not mechanically computable: given~$x$,
determine whether $\TM_x$ outputs a~$7$ for any input.
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_checker}(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TM_x$ outputs $7$ on some input}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
% We follow the pattern of the prior example.
Assume otherwise, that it is a computable
function, and consider this.
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    7 &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
It is computed by the flowchart on the left.
Because it is intuitively mechanically computable, Church's Thesis gives that
there is a Turing machine whose input-output behavior is $\psi$.
That Turing machine has an index~$e$, so that~$\psi=\phi_e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp08.pdf}
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp09.pdf}
\end{center}
The \smn{} theorem gives 
a family of functions $\phi_{s(e,x)}$ parametrized by $x$.
On the right is a flowchart for an associated machine; again, $x$ is hard-coded
so there is a 
different machine for each value of~$x$.
Then, $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_checker}(s(e,x))=1$.
If $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_checker}$ is 
computable then the composition
$\composed{\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_checker}}{s}$
is computable, so the \HP{} is computably solvable, 
which it isn't.
So $\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_checker}$ is not computable.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:Doubles}
Show that this problem is unsolvable:~given $x$, determining whether $\TMfcn_x$
doubles its input, that is, $\TMfcn_x(y)=2y$ for all~$y$. 
We want to show that this function is not mechanically computable.
\begin{equation*}
  \computablefunction{doubler\_checker}(e)=
  \begin{cases}
    1 &\case{if $\TM_e$ outputs $2x$ for all input $x$}  \\
    0 &\case{otherwise} 
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Assume otherwise. 
This function
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x,y)=
  \begin{cases}
    2y &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \uparrow &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
is computed by the flowchart on the left below.
Because $\psi$ is intutitively mechanically computable,
Church's Thesis says that there
a Turing machine that computes it.
This Turing machine has some index,~$e$, so that $\psi=\phi_e$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp10.pdf}
  \hspace*{2em plus 0.5fil}
  \includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp11.pdf}
\end{center}
Apply the \smn{} theorem to get 
a family of functions $\phi_{s(e,x)}$, associated with the 
flowchart on the right, parametrized by $x$.
Then $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if
$\computablefunction{outputs\_seven\_checker}(s(e,x))=1$.
So the supposition that $\computablefunction{doubler\_checker}$ is 
computable gives that the \HP{} is computable, 
which is wrong.
\end{example}

These examples show that one of the roles that the \HP{}
plays is that it is a touchstone for demonstrating algorithmic unsolvability.
Most proofs that something is unsolvable involve showing that
if we could solve the given problem then we could solve
the \HP{}, but we cannot.

We close with two comments.
The first is to reiterate that when we say that a problem is
unsolvable we mean that it is unsolvable
by a mechanism, that there  
is no Turing machine that can compute the solution to the problem.
There can be a \citetext{function that solves it}{\autocite{wiki:ouija}}
but no computable function that solves it.

The other comment is some intuition.
There certainly are problems that start with ``Given an index~$x$'' 
that we can solve.
Just to list one: given~$e$ decide if one instruction in $\TM_e$ is 
$\tminstruction{0}{B}{L}{1}$.
But that problem has a different character than the ones above.
The problems above are about the behavior of the computed function\Dash
each is about $\phi_e$ rather than~$\TM_e$.
This echos the opening of the first chapter,
which says that we are more
interested in the input-output behavior of the machines than in their
implementation details. 
The next subsection expands. 




\subsection{Rice's Theorem}
The intuition from the unsolvability examples is that
we cannot, given $x$,  mechanically analyze the behavior of $\TM_x$.
We now make that intuition precise.
These two definitions formalize `behavior'.

%<*def:SameBehavior>
\begin{definition}
Two computable functions
\definend{have the same behavior $\TMfcn_{e}\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$}
if they converge on the same inputs~$x\in\N$
and, when they do converge, they have the same outputs. 
\end{definition}
%</def:SameBehavior>

%<*def:IndexSet>
\begin{definition}
A set $\mathcal{I}$ of natural numbers is an \definend{index set} when for all 
indices~$e,\hat{e}\in\N$,
if $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and $\TMfcn_e\samebehavior\TMfcn_{\hat{e}}$ 
then also~$\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$. 
\end{definition}
%</def:IndexSet>

\begin{example}
This set of indices
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=2x$ for all $x$}}$
is an index set. 
Suppose that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and that $\hat{e}\in\N$ is such that 
$\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$. 
Then the behavior of $\phi_{\hat{e}}$ is also to double its input: 
$\phi_{\hat{e}}(x)=2x$ for all~$x$.
Thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$ also.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
The set
$\mathcal{J}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat
      \text{$\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$, or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$}}
$
is an index set.
For, suppose that $e\in\mathcal{J}$ and that  
$\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$  where $\hat{e}\in\N$.
Because $e\in\mathcal{J}$, either 
$\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$ or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$.
Because $\phi_e\samebehavior \phi_{\hat{e}}$ we know that
either $\phi_e(x)=3x$ for all $x$ or $\phi_e(x)=x^3$ for all~$x$, and  
thus $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{J}$.
\end{example}

\begin{example} \label{ex:NotIndexSet}
The set $\set{e\in\N\suchthat
      \text{$\TM_e$ contains an instruction starting with $q_{10}$}}$ 
is not an index set.
We can easily produce two Turing machines having the same behavior
where one machine contains such an instruction while the other does not.
\end{example}

%<*th:RicesTheorem>
\begin{theorem}[Rice's Theorem]   \label{thm:RicesThm}
Every index set that is not trivial, that is not empty and not 
all of $\N$, is not computable.
\end{theorem}
%</th:RicesTheorem>

\begin{proof}
%<*pf:RicesTheoremi>
Let $\mathcal{I}$ be an index set.
Choose an~$e_0\in\N$ so that $\phi_{e_0}(y)\diverges$ for all~$y$.
Then either $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$ or $e_0\notin\mathcal{I}$.
We shall show that in the second case $\mathcal{I}$ is not computable. 
The first case is similar and is \cref{exer:OtherHalfRicesThm}.

So assume $e_0\notin\mathcal{I}$.
Since $\mathcal{I}$ is not empty there is an index~$e_1\in\mathcal{I}$.
Because $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set $\phi_{e_0}\not\simeq\phi_{e_1}$
and there is an input~$y$ such that $\phi_{e_1}(y)\converges$.

Consider the flowchart on the left below.
(Note that $e_1$ is not an input, it is hard-coded into the source.)
By Church's Thesis there is a Turing machine with that behavior,
let it be~$\TM_e$.
Apply the \smn{} theorem to paramatrize~$x$,
resulting in the uniformily computable 
family of functions $\phi_{s(e,x)}$, whose behavior is 
charted on the right.
%</pf:RicesTheoremi>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp12.pdf}}
  \hspace{4em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/hp/hp13.pdf}}
\end{center}
%<*pf:RicesTheoremii>
We've constructed the functions on the right so that if 
$\phi_x(x)\diverges$ then $\phi_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\phi_{e_0}$
and thus $s(e,x)\notin\mathcal{I}$, and also so that
if $\phi_x(x)\converges$ then $\phi_{s(e,x)}\samebehavior\phi_{e_1}$
and thus $s(e,x)\in\mathcal{I}$.
Therefore being able to check whether an index $s(e,x)$
is an element of~$\mathcal{I}$
would allow us to solve the \HP.
%</pf:RicesTheoremii>
\end{proof}


\begin{example}
Show using Rice's Theorem that this problem is unsolvable: 
given $e$, decide if $\phi_e(3)\converges$.    

Consider the set
$
  \mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \phi_e(3)\converges}
$.
To apply Rice's Theorem we must show that this set is not empty, that 
it is not all of~$\N$, and that it is an index set.
This set is not empty because we can write a Turing machine that 
acts as the identity function $\phi(x)=x$, and if $e_0$ is the index of that 
Turing Machine then $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$.
The set is not all of $\N$ because, 
where $e_1$ is the index of a Turing Machine that never halts, 
we have that $e_1\notin\mathcal{I}$.

To show that~$\mathcal{I}$ is an index set, assume that
$e\in\mathcal{I}$ and 
let $\hat{e}\in\N$ be such that $\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
Then $e\in\mathcal{I}$ gives that  $\phi_e(3)\converges$ and 
$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$ gives that
$\phi_{\hat{e}}(3)\converges$ also.
Hence $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$, and it is an index set.  
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Show that this problem is unsolvable:
given $e$, decide if $\phi_e(x)=7$ for some~$x$.    

We will show that
$\mathcal{I}=\set{e\in\N \suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=7$ for some $x$}}$
is a nontrivial index set.
It is not empty because, where $e_0$ is the index of a 
Turing Machine that acts as the identity function $\phi_{e_0}(x)=x$,
the input $x=7$ produces the output~$7$
so $e_0\in\mathcal{I}$.
This set is not all of $\N$ because, 
where $e_1$ is the index of a Turing Machine that never halts, 
$e_i\notin\mathcal{I}$.
So this set is nontrivial.

Showing that $\mathcal{I}$ is an index set is straightforward.
Assume that $e\in\mathcal{I}$ and let $\hat{e}\in\N$ be such that
$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
By the first assumption, $\phi_e(x_0)=7$ for some input~$x_0$.
By the second,
$\phi_{\hat{e}}(x_0)=7$ also. 
Consequently, $\hat{e}\in\mathcal{I}$.
\end{example}

We close by reflecting on the significance of Rice's Theorem.
Rice's Theorem speaks to the behavior of the machines.
It addresses the properties of the functions
associated with the machines.
But it does not speak about the properties of the machines
that aren't about the functions; 
for example,~the set of functions
computed by C~programs whose $100$-th character
is a `k' would not be an index set. 

Thus, Rice's Theorem is about understanding what the machines do.
Remember that we declared, in the first paragraph of the first chapter 
of this book,
that we are more interested in what the machines do than in their
internal construction.

At the start of this chapter we saw by a counting argument that 
unsolvable problems exist.
Later the \HP{} showed us that there are some interesting
problems that are unsolvable, some problems
that are significant for the kinds of things
we want to compute in practice.
Here we saw the definition of index set, 
which is the natural way to encapsulate a behavior of interest,
and Rice's Theorem says that for every nontrivial index set,
computing the characteristic function is 
unsolvable.
In this sense, 
Rice's Theorem says that \emph{every} interesting problem
is unsolvable.

% This takes on particular significance in the light of Church's Thesis,
% since it says that `behavior of interest' is any behavior of this type
% that we will ever see in any mechanism.

Now, the prior paragraph is an overstatement; we've all seen
and written real-world programs with behaviors that interested us.
Nonetheless, Rice's Theorem is especially significant
for an understanding what can be done
mechanically.
  

\begin{exercises}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false?
\begin{exparts}
\item
The problem of determining, given~$e$, whether $\TMfcn_e(3)\converges$ is
unsolvable because no function
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$
exists.
\item
The existence of unsolvable problems indicates weaknesses in the
models of comptuation, and we need stronger models.
\item
Rice's Theorem says that every nontrivial problem is unsolvable.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
False.
The function exists.
We say that the problem is unsolvable because that function is not
mechanically computable.
\item
False.
Church's Thesis asserts that Turing machines are a maximally-powerful
computer model.
The existence of unsolvable problems is inherient in the nature of computation.
\item
False.
Rice's Theorem says that every nontrivial problem of a certain kind
(about index sets) is unsolvable.
There are plenty of solvable problems.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:ShowUnsolvableHP}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by showing that solving it 
would enable us to solve the \HP.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem of deciding if a function is the squaring function
$x\mapsto x^2$.
\item The problem of determining if a function returns the same value
on two consecutive inputs, so $f(x)=f(x+1)$ for some~$x\in\N$.
\item The problem of determining if a function is total, that is,
converges on every input.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by showing that solving it 
would enable us to solve the \HP.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem of determining if a function is partial, that is,
fails to converge on some input.
\item The problem of deciding if the function ever converges, on any input.  
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given an index~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
computed by machine~$\TM_e$ is the function
$x\mapsto x+1$.
\item Given an index~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
fails to converge on input~$5$.
\item Given an index~$e$, decide if the function~$\phi_e$
fails to converge on successive inputs $x$ and~$x+1$.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, if it is mechanically solvable
then sketch a program to solve it.
If it is unsolvable then show that.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given $e$, determine the number of states in $\TM_e$.
\item Given $e$, determine whether $\TM_e$ halts when the input is the
  empty string.
\item Given $e$, determine if $\TM_e$ halts on input~$e$ within one
  hundred steps. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This is solvable.
Given the index~$e$, start by getting the source code, $\TM_e$.
Now collect the states that are mentioned.
\item This is solvable; $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$
is not mechanically computable.
For, consider this function. 
\begin{equation*}
  \psi(x)=
  \begin{cases}
    0          &\case{if $\phi_x(x)\converges$} \\
    \diverges &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*} 
It is intuitively mechanically computable, and so by Church's Thesis 
there is a Turing machine that computes it.
Let the index of that machine be~$e$.
Apply the \smn~theorem to parametrize~$x$,
giving a uniformily computable family of functions~$\phi_{s(e,x)}$.
(Note that the $\phi_{s(e,x)}$ don't read any input.)
We have that $\phi_x(x)\converges$ if and only if 
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}(s(e,x))=1$.
If $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$ were mechancially computable
then we could mechanically solve the \HP,
so $\computablefunction{halts\_on\_empty}$ is not mechancially computable.
\item This is decidable.
From the index~$e$, construct~$\TM_e$.
Simulate running it for the hundred steps; in that time it either halts 
or not.     
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an unsolvable problem by applying
Rice's Theorem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem of deciding if a function is the squaring function
$x\mapsto x^2$.
\item The problem of determining if a function returns the same value
on two consecutive inputs, so $f(x)=f(x+1)$ for some~$x\in\N$.
\item The problem of determining if a function is total, that is,
converges on every input.
\item The problem of determining if a function is partial, that is,
fails to converge on some input.
% \item The problem of deciding if the function ever converges, on any input.  
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each problem in \cref{exer:ShowUnsolvableHP}, show it is unsolvable
using Rice's Theorem.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is 
$\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ runs for at least $100$ steps on input~$5$}}$
an index set?
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each problem is unsolvable, using Rice's Theorem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The problem of deciding, given $e\in\N$, whether $\TM_e$ accepts an
infinite language. 
\item
The problem of deciding, given $e\in\N$, whether $\TM_e$ accepts the string
\str{101}.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give an example of a computable function, which is total,
whose range is not computable.
\begin{answer}
Fix an element $k\in K$.
The range of this function
\begin{equation*}
  f(x,n)=
  \begin{cases}
  x  &\case{if $\TM_x$ halts in $n$ steps}  \\
  k  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
is $K$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show that each of these is an index set.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{machine $\TM_e$ halts on at least five inputs}}$
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{the language accepted by $\TM_e$ is the empty language}}$
\item
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{the function $\TMfcn_e$ is one-to-one}}$

\begin{exercise}
Consider the relation $\simeq$ between natural numbers 
given by $e\mathbin{\simeq}\hat{e}$ 
if $\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
\begin{exparts} 
\item
Show that it is an equivalence.
\item 
Describe the equivalence classes.
\item
Show that each index set is the union of some of the equivalence class.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
It is reflexive because a Turing machine $\TM_e$ 
has the same behavior as itself:~$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_e$.
It is symmetric because if $\TM_e$ has the same 
behavior as $\TM_{\hat{e}}$ then the converse also holds.
Transitivity is just as clear.
\item
Each equivalence class~$\mathcal{C}$ is a set of program indices,
where $e,\hat{e}\in\mathcal{C}$ if, while the programs may differ
$\TM_e\neq\TM_{\hat{e}}$, nontheless the 
input-ouptut behaviors are the same~$\phi_e\samebehavior\phi_{\hat{e}}$.
For instance, one class contains the indices of all TM's that double 
their input, 
$\mathcal{C}=\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{$\phi_e(x)=2x$ for all $x$}}$.
\item
The picture below shows the universe of all indices, $\N$, partitioned into
the infinitely many $\simeq$-equivalence classes. 
An index set is a union of some of these classes.
Shown here are three of those classes; their union
makes a single index set
$\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{C}_{e_0}\cup\mathcal{C}_{e_1}\cup\mathcal{C}_{e_2}$.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{background/asy/indexsets/indexsets.pdf}}     
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% ========================================
\section{Computably enumerable sets}
% Required for the section on oracles, which in turn is required
% for the seciton on barriers to solving P=NP.
Recall from \cref{def:Countable} 
that we say a function $\map{f}{\N}{S}$ enumerates its range.
The intuition is that we are outputting the elements of the set:~$f(0)$, 
then~$f(1)$, etc.
 
The natural attack on the \HP{} is to simulate 
running $\TM_0$ on input~$0$ for a few
steps, and then simulate $\TM_1$ on input~$1$ for a few steps.
Then run $\TM_0$ on~$0$ for a more few steps,
followed by $\TM_1$ on~$1$ for a few more,
and then $\TM_2$ on~$2$ for a few steps.
Keep cycling among the $\TM_e$ on~$e$ simulations,
running each for a few steps before going on.
Over time some of these will halt and so
the elements of $K$ will start to fill in.
On computer systems this interleaving is called time-slicing 
but in theory discussions it is called 
\citetext{\definend{dovetailing}}{% https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Joinery-throughdovetail.svg
  A dovetail joint is often used in woodworking, 
  for example to hold together the sides of a drawer.
  It weaves the two sides in alternately, as shown here.
  \protect\begin{center}
  \protect\small
  \protect\includegraphics[width=0.25\textwidth]{background/pix/dovetail.png} 
  \protect\end{center}}.


Why won't this scheme of gradual enumeration
allow us to decide membership in~$K$?
The problem is with $K$'s complement.
Enumeration will tell us what is in~$K$\Dash if $e\in K$ then we 
will find that out eventually\Dash but if $e\notin K$ then 
the computation never halts and
we just wait forever.

%<*def:ComputableSet>
\begin{definition}
A set of natural numbers
is \definend{decidable},
or \definend{computable}, if its
characteristic function is computable, 
if we can mechanically determine both membership and non-membership.
\end{definition}
%</def:ComputableSet>

%<*def:ComputablyEnumerable>
\begin{definition}
A set is \definend{computably enumerable} (or \definend{recursively enumerable},
or \definend{c.e.}, or \definend{r.e.})
if it is effectively listable, that is, if it is
the set of outputs of some computable function
\definend{$W_e=\set{k\suchthat \text{$k=\TMfcn_e(i)$ for some $i\in\N$}}$}.
\end{definition}
%</def:ComputablyEnumerable>

Picture a stream of numbers generated by a computable function
$\TMfcn_e(0)$, $\TMfcn_e(1)$, $\TMfcn_e(2)$,~\ldots{}, gradually filling 
out the set~$W_e$.
Note that a computably enumerable set can be empty, if
$\TMfcn_e$ never converges for any input.

%<*description:DecidableAndSemidecidable>
A set~$S$ is computable if and only if there is a
Turing machine that decides membership:~given an input number~$x$,
the machine decides either `yes' or `no' whether $x\in S$.
In contrast,
with computably enumerable sets there is a Turing machine that
decides `yes' 
but there is no requirement that the machine also decides `no'.
Restated, a set is computable if there is a Turing machine that 
recognizes both members and nonmembers, while
a set is computably enumerable if there is a Turing machine that
recognizes members.
Computably enumerable sets are sometimes called 
\definend{semidecidable}.

One reason that the computably enumerable sets are interesting is 
philosophical;~with spurred by Church's Thesis we think that 
these are the only sets 
that we will, in a sense, ever know.
%</description:DecidableAndSemidecidable>

\begin{lemma}  \label{lem:ComputableAndCE}
\begin{thmparts}
\item \label{lem:SetComputableThenSetCE}
%<*lem:ComputableAndCEi>
  If a set is computable then it is computably enumerable.
%</lem:ComputableAndCEi>
\item \label{lem:SetComputableIffSetAndCompCE}
%<*lem:ComputableAndCEii>
  A set is computable if and only if both it and its
  complement are computably enumerable.
%</lem:ComputableAndCEii>
\end{thmparts}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For the first item let the set~$S\subseteq\N$ be computable
and we will produce an effective enumeration~$\map{f}{\N}{\N}$.
If $S$ is finite then take $f(0)=s_0$, $f(1)=s_1$, \ldots
$f(n-1)=s_{n-1}$, and for any larger input $m\geq n$, define $f(m)\diverges$.
The other case is that $S$ is infinite.
For~$f(0)$, find the smallest element of~$S$ 
by testing whether $0\in S$, then whether $1\in S$, \ldots\@ 
This unbounded search is effective because~$S$ is computable
and it must halt because $S$ is infinite.
Similarly, define $f(k)$ to be the $k$-th smallest element in~$S$.

As to the second item, first
suppose that $S$ is computable.
The prior item shows that it is computably enumerable.
Since the complement of a computable set is also computable,
the prior item also
gives that $\setcomp{S}$ is computably enumerable.

For the other direction
suppose that both $S$ and $\setcomp{S}$ are computably enumerable,
and that $S$ is enumerated by~$f_S$ while $\setcomp{S}$ is
enumerated by $f_{\setcomp{S}}$.
Given an input~$x$,
effectively determine which of the two sets contains~$x$ by
dovetailing their enumerations.
That is, first run the computation of $f_{S}(0)$ for a step,
and the computation of $f_{\setcomp{S}}(0)$ for a step.
Then run the computations of $f_{S}(0)$ and
$f_{\setcomp{S}}(0)$ for a second step, etc.
The sets are complements so eventually $x$ is enumerated into one or the other.
\end{proof}

%<*lem:HPIsCE>
\begin{lemma}
The \HP{} set $K$ is computably enumerable.
Its complement $\setcomp{K}$ is not.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:HPIsCE>

\begin{proof}
The set $K$ is the domain of the function
$f(x)=\TMfcn_x(x)$,
which is mechanically computable by Church's Thesis.
If the complement~$\setcomp{K}$ were computably enumerable then
\cref{lem:ComputableAndCE} would imply that $K$ is computable
but it is not computable.
\end{proof}

That result gives one more reason to be interested in computable
enumerability sets, in that the
The \HP{} set~$K$ falls into the class of computably enumerable sets,
as do sets such as 
$\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$
and $\set{e\suchthat \text{there is an $x$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$. 

%<*lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn}
A set is computably enumerable if and only if it is the domain of a
partial computable function.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:CEIffDomainOfComputableFcn>

\begin{proof}
Assume first that $S$ is computably enumerable, so it 
is the range of a computable function~$f$\!.
We will produce an effective~$\map{g}{\N}{\N}$ that converges if and only
if the input is an element of~$S$.
Given the input $x\in\N$,
enumerate the outputs $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots, and wait for~$x$ to appear.
If it does appear then halt and return some nominal value.
If it never appears then this procedure never halts.

The implication in the other direction is more involved.
Assume that $S$ is the domain of the partial computable function~$g$.
If $S$ is empty then it is computably enumerable by definition.
So assume that~$S$ is not empty and fix some $\hat{s}\in S$. 
We will produce a computable function~$f$ whose range is~$S$. 
Given input $n\in \N$, run the computations of 
$g(0)$, $g(1)$, \ldots\,$g(n)$ for $n$-many steps each.
Possibly some of these computations halt.
Define $f(n)$ to be the least $k$ so $g(k)$ halts within~$n$ steps and such
that $k\notin\set{f(0),f(1),\ldots f(n-1)}$.
If no such $k$ exists then define $f(n)=\hat{s}$.

If $s\in S$ then eventually $g(s)$ must converge,
in some number~$n_s$ of steps.
At that point the number~$s$ is queued for output by~$f$ in the sense that
it will be enumerated by~$f$ as, at most, $f(n_s+s)$. 
\end{proof}


\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Give a function that enumerates each set.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\N$
\item the even numbers
\item the perfect squares
\item the set $\set{5,7,11}$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
For each part we must produce a $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ that is effective. 
\begin{exparts}
\item The identity function, $f(x)=x$, works.
\item The first one that comes to mind is $f(x)=2x$.
\item $f(x)=x^2$
\item This will do.
\begin{equation*}
   f(x)=
   \begin{cases}
     5  &\case{if $x=0$}  \\
     7  &\case{if $x=1$}  \\
    11  &\case{otherwise}
   \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give a function that enumerates each set
\begin{exparts*}
\item the prime numbers
\item the natural numbers where the digits come in non-increasing order
  (e.g., $531$ or $5331$ but not $513$).
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
For each part we must produce a $\map{f}{\N}{\N}$ that is effective. 
\begin{exparts}
\item Clearly we can write a program that inputs~$n$ and outputs the $n$-th
  prime number.
\item One approach is brute force: to generate the $n$-th one just start
  searching through the natural numbers, $0$, $1$, \ldots\,, checking
  each to see if the digits appear in non-increasing order.
  Then just output the $n$-th one that you generate.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
You got a quiz question to define computably enumerable.
A friend of yours says they answered with, 
``A set that can be enumerated by a Turing machine
but that is not computable.''
Is your friend right?
\begin{answer}
Not completely right.
A set is computably enumerable if it can be enumerated by a Turing machine, all
right.
That is, a Turing machine exists that, when started on a blank tape, will
print all of the sets members on the tape (it may run forever, if there are
infinitely many set members).
However, the ``but is not computable'' is wrong.
Any computable set is computably enumerable, although there are 
computably enumerable sets that are not computable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
One of these two is computable and the other is computably enumerable.
Which is which?
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$4$ in less than twenty steps}}$
\item $\set{e\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input~$4$ in more than twenty steps}}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
The first is computable, since to decide if a number~$e$ is a member of 
this set we can run a simulation for twenty steps.
The second is computably enumerable but not computable.
It is clearly enumerable since we can dovetail computations for the indices
$e\in\N$.
It is not computable because clearly if we could solve it then we could 
solve the \HP.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Which of these sets is decidable, which is semidecidable but not decidable, 
and which is neither?
Justify in one sentence.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes more than $100$ steps
on input~$7$.
\item
The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes less than $100$ steps
on input~$7$.
\item
The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes more than $100$ steps
on every input.
\item
The set of indices~$e$ such that $\TM_e$ takes less than $100$ steps
on all inputs.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
This set is decidable, since we can just run the machine for the stated number
of steps.
Because it is decidable, it is also semidecidable.
\item 
This set is also decidable, again since we can just run the machine
for the stated number of steps.
Because it is decidable, it is semidecidable.
\item
This set is neither.
Testing all inputs would never halt.
\item
As with the prior item, this set is neither.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Can there be a set such that the problem of determining membership in that
set is unsolvable, and also the set is computably enumerable?
\begin{answer}
Yes, the set~$K$ fits both criteria.
Three other examples are the set
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$,
the set 
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$,
and $\set{e\in\N\suchthat\text{$TMfcn_e(x)=2x$ for all $x\in\N$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Short answer:~for each set state whether it is decidable, 
semidecidable (but not decidable), or neither.
Give a brief justification.
\begin{exparts}
\item The set of indices~$e$ of Turing machines that contain an instruction using
state~$q_4$.
\item The set of indices of Turing machines that halt on input~$3$.
\item The set of indices of Turing machines that halt on input~$3$ in fewer than
 $100$ steps.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Decidable.
Indices are source-equivalent: there is a program that takes in the index~$e$
and puts out the set of instructions.  
With that set, just scan it for~$q_4$,
\item Semidecidable but not decidable.
Use dovetailing to
run $\TM_e$ on input~$3$ for all the $e$'s.
When a the machines halts, enumerate~$e$.
As to being not decidable, we know from the prior section that this is not 
a computable set.
\item Computable.
Given~$e$, decide if it is in the set by running~$\TM_e$ on~$3$ for the
hundred steps.
The computation either halts or it does not.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
You read someone online who says, 
``every countable set~$S$ is computably enumerable because if
$\map{f}{\N}{S}$ then you have the enumeration $S$ as $f(0)$, $f(1)$, \ldots\@''
Explain why that argument is wrong.
\begin{answer}
It is an enumeration, but unless the function is computable it is not
a computable enumeration.
The complement of~$K$ is countable but is not computable enumerable. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Name three sets that are computably enumerable but not computable.
\begin{answer}
There are of course many correct answers.
One way to answer the question is to first take~$K$.
For the second set, take~$K$ with its smallest element omitted.
For the third set, take~$K$ with the smallest two elements omitted. 

A perhaps less wiseguy-ish answer is to take~$K$ along with some sets
that we used as examples in the prior section, such as 
$\set{e\in\N\suchthat \TMfcn_e(3)\converges}$
and $\set{e\in\N\suchthat \text{there is $x\in\N$ so that $\TMfcn_e(x)=7$}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Write a program to enumerate each set.
\begin{exparts*}
\item the even numbers
\item the perfect squares
\item the primes
\item the set $\set{5,7,11}$.
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Prove that every finite set is computably enumerable.
\item
Write a program that takes in a finite set and returns
a routine that enumerates the set.
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that the set $A_5=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(5)\converges}$
is computably enumerable.
\item
Generalize the previous item to show that 
$A_k=\set{e\suchthat \TMfcn_e(k)\converges}$
is computably enumerable.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Prove that every infinite computably enumerable set has an infinite computable
subset.
\begin{answer}
Let the computably enumerable set be $W$ 
and fix a computable enumeration $\TMfcn(0)$, $\TMfcn(1)$, \ldots\@.
We will define a computable $S\subseteq W$.
The first element of~$S$ is~$s_0=\TMfcn(0)$.
The second element~$s_1$ is the first member of the enumeration that
is larger than $s_0$; one must eventually appear in the enumeration 
because $W$ is infinite.
In general~$s_i$ is the first element of the enumeration that is larger 
than $s_{i-1}$ (as with $s_1$, one must eventually appear).

The set~$S$ is computably enumerable because we have just described a 
mechanical procedure to enumerate it.
This set is computable because given a number $n\in\N$, to determine
whether it is an element of~$S$ just wait until either it is enumerated 
into~$S$, which shows $n\in S$, 
or a number larger than it is enumerated into~$S$, which shows
$n\notin S$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Suppose that there is a set $A$ and a computable function $f$ such that
the set difference $A-\operatorname{range}(f)$ is finite.
Show that $A$ is computably enumerable.
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Let the set $K_0$ be the set
$\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $x$}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that $K_0$ is computably enumerable.
\item
Show that the columns of $K_0$, the sets
$C_e=\set{\sequence{e,x}\suchthat \text{$\TM_e$ halts on input $x$}}$
make up all the computable enumerable sets.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
This section starts by recalling that we say that a function $\map{f}{\N}{S}$ 
enumerates its range~$R\subseteq S$.
Suppose that the set~$R$ is infinite and that the function is computable.
Show that
if the function is not total then we can produce a total
$\map{\hat{f}}{\N}{R}$ that enumerates~$R$.
\begin{answer}
Dovetail.
That is, suppose that $f$ is computed by $\TM$.
Then $\hat{f}(k)$ is the $k+1$-th number output in the following process.

Generate the pairs using the pairing function, 
the inverse of Cantor's correspondence:
$\pair(0)=\sequence{0,0}$, 
$\pair(1)=\sequence{1,0}$, $\pair(2)=\sequence{0,1}$, etc.
Where the $n$-th one be $\sequence{x,s}$, 
run~$\TM$ on input~$x$ for $s$-many steps.  
If it halts then output its value.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\begin{exercise}
Write a program that will, given two enumerating programs, dovetail them.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \textit{enumerable in nondecreasing order}
if there is a computable function whose range is the set, and
tht is nondecreasing:~$n\leq m$ implies that~$f(n)\leq f(m)$.
Similarly a set is \textit{enumerable in increasing order}
if $n<m$ implies~$f(n)<f(m)$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Prove that a set is computable and nonempty if and only if it is
enumerable in nondecreasing order.
\item 
Prove that a set is computable and infinite if and only if it is
enumerable in increasing order.
\item
Prove that every infinite computably enumerable set has an
infinite computable subset.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \textit{computably enumerable without repetition} if it is
the range of a computable function that is one-to-one.
Prove that a set is computably enumerable and infinite if and only if
it is computably enumerable without repetition.
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
A set is \definend{co-computably enumerable} 
if its complement is computabily enumerable.
Produce a set that is neither computably enumerable nor co-computably 
enumerable.
\begin{answer}
The set of ordered pairs 
$\set{\sequence{a,b}\suchthat 
     \text{$a\in K$ and $b\in\setcomp{K}$}}$ is not
computably enumerable, or else $\setcomp{K}$ would be computably enumerable.
Similarly it is not co-computably enumerable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


% \begin{TODO}
% Exercise based on:
% https://cstheory.stackexchange.com/questions/38193/about-the-decidability-of-sets-enumerated-in-non-decreasing-order?
% \end{TODO}

\begin{exercise}
Computable enumerability is a property of sets so we can consider its
interaction with set operations.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
Must a subset of a computably enumerable set be computably enumerable?
\item
Must the union of two computably enumerable sets be computably enumerable?
\item
The intersection?
\item
The complement?
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}

% I don't know that it is doable here.

% Prompted by the philosophical consideration at the end of the section,
% define a binary relation between integers as $x~e$
% if $x\in W_e$.
% \begin{exparts*}
% \item
% Is this relation reflexive?
% \item
% Symmetric?
% \item
% Transitive?
% \end{exparts*}
